Most days it’s black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little tuna. If I’m not moving freight that shift, I tell myself I don’t really need a full meal. I mean, that makes sense, right?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds a little strong, maybe. I just like to keep an eye on it. If I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or see how my belt feels, just to make sure I haven’t… you know, let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t like saying “fear,” because that makes it sound dramatic, but if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I do start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then I’ll check the scale the next morning, or my belt, just to make sure I haven’t let myself go. Does that… answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess it depends what you’d call significant. The clinic for that logistics job sent me here because they said my weight was too low to clear me. But I don’t know… to me I just look lean, like somebody who’s ready to work and stay quick on his feet. Is that… the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say underweight exactly… I mean, I notice my stomach and my waist mostly. If they don’t look smaller, tight, controlled, then I feel like I’m not the disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. Maybe that sounds strange, but that’s kind of where my eyes go in the mirror.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Is that… the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say underweight exactly… I mean, I notice my stomach and my waist mostly.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, uh… I’m here because the clinic for this logistics job said my weight was too low to clear me. I don’t really see it that way, though. I mean, I’ve always figured I’m just lean—like, ready to work, quick on my feet, you know? But they said I needed to come in and get checked, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose weight exactly… I just try to keep things light, you know, practical. Most days it’s black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little tuna. If I’m not moving freight that shift, I tell myself I don’t really need a full meal. I mean, that makes sense, right?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds a little strong, maybe. I just like to keep an eye on it. If I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or see how my belt feels, just to make sure I haven’t… you know, let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t like saying “fear,” because that makes it sound dramatic, but if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I do start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then I’ll check the scale the next morning, or my belt, just to make sure I haven’t let myself go. Does that… answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess it depends what you’d call significant. The clinic for that logistics job sent me here because they said my weight was too low to clear me. But I don’t know… to me I just look lean, like somebody who’s ready to work and stay quick on his feet. Is that… the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say underweight exactly… I mean, I notice my stomach and my waist mostly. If they don’t look smaller, tight, controlled, then I feel like I’m not the disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. Maybe that sounds strange, but that’s kind of where my eyes go in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m a man, so I don’t have a menstrual cycle. Unless you mean some other kind of hormone thing? I’m sorry, I just want to make sure I’m answering what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you know anyone, such as a female friend or family member, who has experienced changes in their menstrual cycle, such as skipped periods, as a result of being underweight?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say I know anyone like that, no. I mean, I’ve heard people talk about skipped periods in general, but not someone close to me where I knew it was because of being underweight. Sorry, I don’t want to guess and give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you were a bit heavier\n\nPatient: I want to say yes because I know that probably sounds like the healthier answer… but honestly, I don’t think I’d feel happier. When I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, if I’m not smaller and tight-looking, I feel like I’ve let myself go somehow… like I’m not that disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or eating habits have affected your sex drive or ability to function sexually?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I haven’t really been focusing on that part of things much lately. I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear problem, but… I’m not sure if I’d connect anything directly to my weight or eating. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably. I don’t like admitting that, because I know it sounds kind of… not right.\n\nIf I wasn’t worried about gaining weight, I think I’d be able to eat more normal things. Like rice or bread, or something greasy, I guess. But when I do, I can’t stop thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale or even my belt, just to make sure I didn’t… let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does worrying about gaining weight interfere with your daily activities or thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does, more than I’d like to admit. I try not to make a big deal of it, but if I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, it kind of stays in my head. I’ll be thinking, “That’s going to sit on me,” like right away.\n\nAnd then the next morning I’ll usually check the scale, or even how my belt feels, just to make sure I didn’t… you know, let myself go. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s what happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s just, if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or how my belt feels, just to make sure I didn’t let myself go. So… yeah, I guess the fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I hold back. I mean, I’ve always figured I’m just lean—like, ready to work, quick on my feet, you know? But they said I needed to come in and get checked, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose weight exactly… I just try to keep things light, you know, practical. Most days it’s black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little tuna. If I’m not moving freight that shift, I tell myself I don’t really need a full meal. I mean, that makes sense, right?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds a little strong, maybe. I just like to keep an eye on it. If I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or see how my belt feels, just to make sure I haven’t… you know, let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t like saying “fear,” because that makes it sound dramatic, but if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I do start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then I’ll check the scale the next morning, or my belt, just to make sure I haven’t let myself go. Does that… answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess it depends what you’d call significant. The clinic for that logistics job sent me here because they said my weight was too low to clear me. Is that… the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say underweight exactly… I mean, I notice my stomach and my waist mostly. If they don’t look smaller, tight, controlled, then I feel like I’m not the disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. Maybe that sounds strange, but that’s kind of where my eyes go in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m a man, so I don’t have a menstrual cycle. I mean… I can see I’m not a big guy right now, sure. I’m not trying to be stubborn about that.

But they said I needed to come in and get checked, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose weight exactly… I just try to keep things light, you know, practical. Most days it’s black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little tuna. If I’m not moving freight that shift, I tell myself I don’t really need a full meal. I mean, that makes sense, right?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds a little strong, maybe. I just like to keep an eye on it. If I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or see how my belt feels, just to make sure I haven’t… you know, let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t like saying “fear,” because that makes it sound dramatic, but if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I do start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then I’ll check the scale the next morning, or my belt, just to make sure I haven’t let myself go. Does that… answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess it depends what you’d call significant. The clinic for that logistics job sent me here because they said my weight was too low to clear me. But I don’t know… to me I just look lean, like somebody who’s ready to work and stay quick on his feet. Is that… the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say underweight exactly… I mean, I notice my stomach and my waist mostly. If they don’t look smaller, tight, controlled, then I feel like I’m not the disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. Maybe that sounds strange, but that’s kind of where my eyes go in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m a man, so I don’t have a menstrual cycle. Unless you mean some other kind of hormone thing? I’m sorry, I just want to make sure I’m answering what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you know anyone, such as a female friend or family member, who has experienced changes in their menstrual cycle, such as skipped periods, as a result of being underweight?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say I know anyone like that, no. I mean, I’ve heard people talk about skipped periods in general, but not someone close to me where I knew it was because of being underweight. Sorry, I don’t want to guess and give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you were a bit heavier\n\nPatient: I want to say yes because I know that probably sounds like the healthier answer… but honestly, I don’t think I’d feel happier. When I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, if I’m not smaller and tight-looking, I feel like I’ve let myself go somehow… like I’m not that disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or eating habits have affected your sex drive or ability to function sexually?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I haven’t really been focusing on that part of things much lately. I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear problem, but… I’m not sure if I’d connect anything directly to my weight or eating. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably. I don’t like admitting that, because I know it sounds kind of… not right.\n\nIf I wasn’t worried about gaining weight, I think I’d be able to eat more normal things. Like rice or bread, or something greasy, I guess. But when I do, I can’t stop thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale or even my belt, just to make sure I didn’t… let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does worrying about gaining weight interfere with your daily activities or thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does, more than I’d like to admit. I try not to make a big deal of it, but if I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, it kind of stays in my head. I’ll be thinking, “That’s going to sit on me,” like right away.\n\nAnd then the next morning I’ll usually check the scale, or even how my belt feels, just to make sure I didn’t… you know, let myself go. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but that’s probably the main thing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be difficult about food. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or how my belt feels, just to make sure I didn’t let myself go. So… yeah, I guess the fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I hold back. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others tell you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sometimes. I’m just trying to explain it honestly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is low even when you look at yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can see I’m not a big guy right now, sure. But they said I needed to come in and get checked, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose weight exactly… I just try to keep things light, you know, practical. Most days it’s black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little tuna. If I’m not moving freight that shift, I tell myself I don’t really need a full meal. I mean, that makes sense, right?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds a little strong, maybe. I just like to keep an eye on it. If I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or see how my belt feels, just to make sure I haven’t… you know, let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t like saying “fear,” because that makes it sound dramatic, but if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I do start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then I’ll check the scale the next morning, or my belt, just to make sure I haven’t let myself go. Does that… answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess it depends what you’d call significant. The clinic for that logistics job sent me here because they said my weight was too low to clear me. But I don’t know… to me I just look lean, like somebody who’s ready to work and stay quick on his feet. Is that… the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say underweight exactly… I mean, I notice my stomach and my waist mostly. If they don’t look smaller, tight, controlled, then I feel like I’m not the disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. Maybe that sounds strange, but that’s kind of where my eyes go in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m a man, so I don’t have a menstrual cycle. Unless you mean some other kind of hormone thing? I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or eating habits have affected your sex drive or ability to function sexually?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, uh… I’m here because the clinic for this logistics job said my weight was too low to clear me. I don’t really see it that way, though. I mean, I’ve always figured I’m just lean—like, ready to work, quick on my feet, you know? But they said I needed to come in and get checked, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose weight exactly… I just try to keep things light, you know, practical. Most days it’s black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little tuna. If I’m not moving freight that shift, I tell myself I don’t really need a full meal. I mean, that makes sense, right?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds a little strong, maybe. I just like to keep an eye on it. If I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or see how my belt feels, just to make sure I haven’t… you know, let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t like saying “fear,” because that makes it sound dramatic, but if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I do start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then I’ll check the scale the next morning, or my belt, just to make sure I haven’t let myself go. Does that… answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess it depends what you’d call significant. The clinic for that logistics job sent me here because they said my weight was too low to clear me. But I don’t know… to me I just look lean, like somebody who’s ready to work and stay quick on his feet. Is that… the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say underweight exactly… I mean, I notice my stomach and my waist mostly. If they don’t look smaller, tight, controlled, then I feel like I’m not the disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. Maybe that sounds strange, but that’s kind of where my eyes go in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m a man, so I don’t have a menstrual cycle. Unless you mean some other kind of hormone thing? I’m sorry, I just want to make sure I’m answering what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you know anyone, such as a female friend or family member, who has experienced changes in their menstrual cycle, such as skipped periods, as a result of being underweight?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say I know anyone like that, no. I mean, I’ve heard people talk about skipped periods in general, but not someone close to me where I knew it was because of being underweight. Sorry, I don’t want to guess and give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you were a bit heavier\n\nPatient: I want to say yes because I know that probably sounds like the healthier answer… but honestly, I don’t think I’d feel happier. When I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, if I’m not smaller and tight-looking, I feel like I’ve let myself go somehow… like I’m not that disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or eating habits have affected your sex drive or ability to function sexually?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I haven’t really been focusing on that part of things much lately. I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear problem, but… I’m not sure if I’d connect anything directly to my weight or eating. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably. I don’t like admitting that, because I know it sounds kind of… not right.\n\nIf I wasn’t worried about gaining weight, I think I’d be able to eat more normal things. Like rice or bread, or something greasy, I guess. But when I do, I can’t stop thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale or even my belt, just to make sure I didn’t… let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does worrying about gaining weight interfere with your daily activities or thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does, more than I’d like to admit. I try not to make a big deal of it, but if I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, it kind of stays in my head. I’ll be thinking, “That’s going to sit on me,” like right away.\n\nAnd then the next morning I’ll usually check the scale, or even how my belt feels, just to make sure I didn’t… you know, let myself go. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but that’s probably the main thing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s just, if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or how my belt feels, just to make sure I didn’t let myself go. So… yeah, I guess the fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I hold back. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others tell you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sometimes. I know people say I’m underweight, and I’m not trying to argue with them exactly.\n\nBut when I look in the mirror, especially around my stomach and waist, it doesn’t look right to me. If I’m not smaller there, kind of tight-looking, I feel like I’ve lost discipline… like I’m not the same man I used to be when I was working hard on the warehouse floor.\n\nI know that may not make sense from the outside. I’m just trying to explain it honestly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is low even when you look at yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can see I’m not a big guy right now, sure. I’m not trying to be stubborn about that.\n\nBut when my sister and the doctor say I’m in danger, I guess part of me feels like they’re making it bigger than it is. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, uh… I’m here because the clinic for this logistics job said my weight was too low to clear me. I don’t really see it that way, though. I mean, I’ve always figured I’m just lean—like, ready to work, quick on my feet, you know? But they said I needed to come in and get checked, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose weight exactly… I just try to keep things light, you know, practical. Most days it’s black coffee, maybe a boiled egg or a little tuna. If I’m not moving freight that shift, I tell myself I don’t really need a full meal. I mean, that makes sense, right?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds a little strong, maybe. I just like to keep an eye on it. If I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or see how my belt feels, just to make sure I haven’t… you know, let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t like saying “fear,” because that makes it sound dramatic, but if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I do start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then I’ll check the scale the next morning, or my belt, just to make sure I haven’t let myself go. Does that… answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess it depends what you’d call significant. The clinic for that logistics job sent me here because they said my weight was too low to clear me. But I don’t know… to me I just look lean, like somebody who’s ready to work and stay quick on his feet. Is that… the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say underweight exactly… I mean, I notice my stomach and my waist mostly. If they don’t look smaller, tight, controlled, then I feel like I’m not the disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. Maybe that sounds strange, but that’s kind of where my eyes go in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m a man, so I don’t have a menstrual cycle. Unless you mean some other kind of hormone thing? I’m sorry, I just want to make sure I’m answering what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you know anyone, such as a female friend or family member, who has experienced changes in their menstrual cycle, such as skipped periods, as a result of being underweight?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say I know anyone like that, no. I mean, I’ve heard people talk about skipped periods in general, but not someone close to me where I knew it was because of being underweight. Sorry, I don’t want to guess and give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you were a bit heavier\n\nPatient: I want to say yes because I know that probably sounds like the healthier answer… but honestly, I don’t think I’d feel happier. When I look in the mirror, especially at my stomach and waist, if I’m not smaller and tight-looking, I feel like I’ve let myself go somehow… like I’m not that disciplined guy I used to be on the warehouse floor. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or eating habits have affected your sex drive or ability to function sexually?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I haven’t really been focusing on that part of things much lately. I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear problem, but… I’m not sure if I’d connect anything directly to my weight or eating. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably. I don’t like admitting that, because I know it sounds kind of… not right.\n\nIf I wasn’t worried about gaining weight, I think I’d be able to eat more normal things. Like rice or bread, or something greasy, I guess. But when I do, I can’t stop thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale or even my belt, just to make sure I didn’t… let myself go. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does worrying about gaining weight interfere with your daily activities or thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does, more than I’d like to admit. I try not to make a big deal of it, but if I eat something like rice or bread, or anything greasy, it kind of stays in my head. I’ll be thinking, “That’s going to sit on me,” like right away.\n\nAnd then the next morning I’ll usually check the scale, or even how my belt feels, just to make sure I didn’t… you know, let myself go. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but that’s probably the main thing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s just, if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Then the next morning I’ll check the scale, or how my belt feels, just to make sure I didn’t let myself go. So… yeah, I guess the fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I hold back. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others tell you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sometimes. I know people say I’m underweight, and I’m not trying to argue with them exactly.\n\nBut when I look in the mirror, especially around my stomach and waist, it doesn’t look right to me. If I’m not smaller there, kind of tight-looking, I feel like I’ve lost discipline… like I’m not the same man I used to be when I was working hard on the warehouse floor.\n\nI know that may not make sense from the outside. I’m just trying to explain it honestly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is low even when you look at yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can see I’m not a big guy right now, sure. I’m not trying to be stubborn about that.\n\nBut when my sister and the doctor say I’m in danger, I guess part of me feels like they’re making it bigger than it is.
